A quarter-bound book with a light-colored vellum or leather spine and textured tan cloth-covered boards. The spine features a small, dark red leather label with gold-stamped lettering.

**Warning:** The lettering on the spine label is small and slightly blurred.

<meta>This page transitions from the classical Roman philosophy of the previous page to the mystical and esoteric traditions of the early modern period. While the previous entry focused on Cicero’s rational inquiry into the divine, this frontispiece introduces "Hermetic Philosophy," a tradition that blended alchemy, mysticism, and spiritual transformation.</meta>

->Hermetic Philosophy<- <note>original Latin: "PHILOSOPHIA · HERMETICA"</note>

<insert>Rv</insert> <note>Likely a shorthand for "Recto" or "Verso," used by a cataloger to denote the side of the leaf on which this emblem is pasted.</note>

**Description of the Emblem**

A circular, colored emblem or bookplate is pasted onto the center of the page. The image is framed by the words "Hermetic Philosophy" in golden-yellow letters. At the center is an upright oval on a blue field, containing several layers of symbolic imagery:

*   **The Pelican:** A white <term>pelican in her piety</term> <note>A traditional motif in which a pelican wounds its own breast to feed its young with its blood. In this context, it symbolizes the alchemical process of "multiplication" or the ultimate sacrifice of the self for spiritual rebirth.</note> sits atop a golden crescent-shaped nest.
*   **The Rose Cross:** Below the pelican is a square block featuring an X-shaped cross with four small red roses in the corners—the hallmark symbol of <term>Rosicrucianism</term> <note>A secret society of mystics and alchemists who sought to combine spiritual wisdom with scientific discovery during the 17th century.</note>.
*   **The Arch:** At the top of the oval, golden rays of light shine down from beneath a green, leafy arch, representing divine illumination.

<term>Hermeticism</term> <note>A philosophical and religious system based on the legendary writings of Hermes Trismegistus, focusing on the hidden laws of nature and the purification of the human soul.</note>

<term>Alchemical Symbolism</term> <note>The use of animals, colors, and shapes to represent the stages of a "Great Work," which aimed to transform both base metals into gold and the human spirit into a perfected state.</note>

<summary>This page features a symbolic emblem for "Hermetic Philosophy," incorporating Rosicrucian and alchemical imagery such as the pelican in her piety and the rose cross. It marks a shift in the manuscript toward esoteric and mystical subjects.</summary>

<keywords>Hermetic Philosophy, Pelican in her piety, Rosicrucianism, Alchemical symbolism, Esotericism, Bookplate</keywords>

<meta>This is the title page for the first volume of the 1742 London edition of "Le Comte de Gabalis." It establishes the satirical yet instructional tone of the work, which claims to reveal the secrets of elemental spirits.</meta>

# THE COUNT
### OF
# GABALIS,
#### OR
## CONVERSATIONS
#### ON
### THE SECRET
### SCIENCES.

**NEW EDITION,**

ENLARGED with the Assisting Genies <note>In the context of 17th-century occultism, "genies" refers to elemental spirits—such as sylphs of the air—rather than the "genies" of Middle Eastern folklore.</note> & the irreconcilable Gnomes. <note>The "Gnomes" here are the elemental spirits of the Earth; the author uses these terms to satirize the complex hierarchies of Rosicrucian philosophy.</note>

*By the Abbot* DE VILLARS. <note>Henri de Montfaucon de Villars (1635–1673) was a French priest. He was later murdered on a road to Lyon; rumors persisted among occultists that he was killed by the "sylphs" for revealing their secrets, though the book is actually a parody.</note>

# THE COUNT

...secrets of the Sages, and that since the Blessed Raymond Lully <note>Raymond Lully (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan philosopher and logician. In the occult tradition, he was falsely credited with numerous alchemical works; the "testament" mentioned here refers to a pseudo-epigraphal text that warned against revealing the secret of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> pronounced the sentence in his testament, an executing Angel has never failed to promptly wring the neck of all those who have indiscreetly revealed the Philosophical Mysteries.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But let them not lightly condemn this learned Man <note>Refers to the Count of Gabalis, the mysterious figure who serves as the narrator’s mentor in these dialogues.</note> without being enlightened as to his conduct. He revealed everything to me, it is true; but he only did so with all the Cabalistic <note>Derived from the Kabbalah, "Cabalistic" in this context refers to the 17th-century Western occult practice of using secret codes, rituals, and strict vetting of students before sharing mystical knowledge.</note> precautions. This testimony must be rendered to his memory: that he was a great zealot for the Religion of his Fathers, the Philosophers, and that he would have suffered the fire rather than profane its sanctity by opening himself to some unworthy Prince, to someone ambitious, or to someone unchaste—three types of people who have been excommunicated for all time by...

He found me to have a docile spirit, curious, and not very timid; I only lack a bit of melancholy <note>In the 17th century, "melancholy" (black bile) was one of the four humors. Far from being a mere sadness, it was believed to be the temperament of genius, deep thought, and those predisposed to the study of the occult.</note> to make all those who would blame Monsieur the Count of Gabalis for hiding nothing from me admit that I was a subject quite suited to the Secret Sciences. Is it impossible that, as the fortunes of war vary from day to day, he succumbed in some combat with some **unruly sprite** <note>original: "lutin indocile." In the world of the occult, "lutins" or sprites were considered elemental spirits that could be mischievous or even dangerous if not properly commanded by a magus.</note>? Perhaps while speaking to God upon his **flaming throne**, he could not keep himself from looking Him in the face; yet it is written that one cannot look upon Him without dying <note>A reference to Exodus 33:20: "But," he said, "you cannot see my face, for no one may see me and live."</note>. Perhaps he only died in appearance, following the custom of the **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to the Hermetic philosophers or Alchemists, who were often rumored to possess the Elixir of Life and to stage their own deaths to move through the centuries unnoticed.</note>, who pretend to die in one place and transplant themselves to another. Common sense having always made me suspect that there is a great deal of emptiness in all that is called <term>Secret Sciences</term> <note>original: "Sciences secretes." In the 17th century, this term referred to occult disciplines like alchemy, astrology, and ceremonial magic.</note>, I have never been tempted to waste time leafing through the books that deal with them; but also, not finding it very reasonable to condemn, without knowing why, all those who devote themselves to them—who are often otherwise **wise** people, mostly **learned**, and of high standing in the **legal profession** <note>original: "la robe." Literally "the robe," referring to the *noblesse de robe*, the class of nobles who held judicial or administrative posts.</note> and the **military** <note>original: "l'épée." Literally "the sword," referring to the *noblesse d'épée*, the traditional knightly aristocracy.</note>—I decided, in order to avoid being unjust and to avoid tiring myself with tedious reading, to pretend to be obsessed with all these sciences in the company of all those whom I could discover were affected by them. I initially had more success than I had even hoped for. Since all these gentlemen,

### OF GABALIS.

however mysterious and however reserved they pride themselves on being, they want nothing more than to display their imaginings and the new discoveries they claim to have made in Nature. In a few days, I became the confidant of the most significant among them; I always had one of them in my **study** <note>original: "cabinet." In the 17th century, this was a private room used for work, study, or the display of collections.</note>, which I had, by design, furnished with their most **fantastic Authors**. No foreign **Scholar** <note>original: "Sçavant."</note> passed through without my being notified; in a word, except for the actual knowledge, I soon found myself a person of great importance. I had as companions Princes, great Lords, **Men of the Robe** <note>original: "Gens de robe." Members of the nobility who held judicial or administrative posts, as opposed to the military nobility.</note>, beautiful Ladies, and ugly ones too; Doctors, Prelates, Monks, Nuns—in short, people of every sort. Some were after the Angels, others the devil, others their **guiding spirit** <note>original: "genie." Derived from the Latin *genius*, referring to a tutelary spirit or supernatural entity that influences a person's life.</note>, others the...

### THE COUNT

**Incubi** <note>In folklore and occult theory, these were malevolent spirits or demons thought to seek sexual contact with sleeping women.</note>, others to the healing of all ailments, others to the **Stars**, others to the secrets of Divinity, and almost all to the **Philosopher’s Stone**.

They all agreed that these great secrets, and especially the **Philosopher’s Stone**, are difficult to find, and that few people possess them; but they each held a sufficiently high opinion of themselves to believe they were among the **Chosen**. Fortunately, the most important among them were then impatiently awaiting the arrival of a German, a great Lord and a great **Cabalist** <note>original: "Cabaliſte." A practitioner of Cabala, a mystical system that 17th-century occultists used to explore the hidden meanings of scripture and the secrets of the universe.</note>, whose lands are near the borders of Poland. He had promised by letter to the **Children of the Philosophers** <note>original: "enfans des Philoſophes." A respectful term for initiates or devoted students of alchemy and the occult sciences.</note> in Paris that he would come to visit them, passing through France on his way to England. I was commissioned to reply to the letter of this great Man;

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrator's account of his correspondence with the mysterious German Cabalist mentioned previously, detailing how he secured the master's favor through intellectual and astrological exchange.</meta>

### OF GABALIS

I sent him the figure of my **nativity** <term>nativity: An astrological birth chart used to determine a person's character and spiritual destiny based on the positions of the stars at their birth.</term>, so that he might judge whether I could aspire to supreme **Wisdom**. My chart and my letter were fortunate enough to oblige him to do me the honor of replying that I would be one of the first he would see in **Paris**; and that, if Heaven did not oppose it, it would not be for lack of effort on his part that I should enter into the **society of the Sages** <note>original: "société des Sages." This refers to a secret brotherhood of initiates, likely the Rosicrucians, who claimed to possess hidden divine knowledge.</note>.

To nurture my good fortune, I maintained a regular correspondence with the illustrious **German**. From time to time, I proposed to him great doubts, as well-reasoned as I could make them, concerning the **Harmony of the world** <note>The ancient concept that the universe is governed by mathematical and musical proportions.</note>, the **Numbers of Pythagoras** <note>Pythagoras taught that numbers were the building blocks of the universe and held mystical significance.</note>, the visions of **Saint John** <note>Specifically the Book of Revelation, which was a primary text for occultists seeking prophetic secrets.</note>, and the first chapter of **Genesis** <note>Esoteric thinkers often looked for hidden meanings regarding the creation of the world in the opening of the Bible.</note>. The grandeur of these subjects delighted him; he wrote me of unheard-of wonders, and I saw clearly that I was dealing with a man—

<summary>The narrator establishes a scholarly rapport with the German Cabalist by sharing his birth chart and engaging in a regular correspondence about sacred geometry, numerology, and biblical mysteries.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, birth chart, Paris, society of the Sages, Pythagoras, Saint John, Genesis, sacred numbers, harmony of the world</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the narrator's description of his intense intellectual exchange with the German Cabalist. The previous page ended mid-sentence: "I saw clearly that I was dealing with a man—"</meta>

### THE COUNT

—man of a very vigorous and very spacious imagination. I have sixty or eighty letters from him in a style so extraordinary that I could no longer resolve to read anything else as soon as I was alone in my **Study** <term>Study: Translated from "cabinet," a small, private room in a 17th-century home used for reading, writing, and the storage of personal collections or important papers.</term>.

I was admiring one of the most sublime of these letters one day, when I saw enter a man of very good appearance, who, greeting me gravely, said to me in the French language and with a foreign accent: "Worship, *my son, worship the very good and very great God of the Sages* <note>original: "Dieu des Sages." This refers to the deity as understood by "enlightened" philosophers and occultists, who believed they possessed a higher, more direct knowledge of the divine than the average person.</note>, *and never pride yourself that he sends you one of the Children of Wisdom* <note>original: "Enfans de Sagesse." A traditional title for those initiated into the secret mysteries of alchemy or the Rosicrucian brotherhood.</note>, *to associate you with their Company, and to make you a participant in the wonders of his Omnipotence.*"

The novelty of this salutation astonished me at first, and I began to doubt for the first time whether one does not sometimes have apparitions <note>The narrator, previously established as a skeptic or "free-thinker," begins to wonder if this mysterious visitor is a supernatural being rather than a physical man.</note>; however...

<summary>The narrator's solitary study of the Cabalist's letters is interrupted by the mysterious arrival of a stranger who greets him as a potential initiate into a secret society. This scene marks the transition from intellectual correspondence to a direct encounter with the mysterious "Count."</summary>
<keywords>Count of Gabalis, Children of Wisdom, God of the Sages, initiation, apparition, secret society, Rosicrucianism</keywords>

->### ON GABALIS<-

...reassuring myself as best I could, and looking at him as civilly as the slight fear I felt would permit me. "Whoever you may be," I said to him, "you whose greeting is not of this world, you do me great honor by coming to visit me: but please allow that, before I worship the **God of the Sages** <term>God of the Sages: In 17th-century occultism, this refers to a supreme being known only to the "enlightened" through secret wisdom, often contrasted with the God of common religious dogma.</term>, I should know <note>original: "sçache" — an archaic spelling of the French word "sache."</note> which Sages and which God you are speaking of; and, if it pleases you, sit in this **armchair** <note>original: "fauteüil." By offering a seat, the narrator attempts to bring the potentially supernatural encounter back down to the level of a polite, earthly social call.</note>, and take the trouble to tell me who this God is, these Sages, this **Company** <note>A reference to the secret brotherhood (likely the Rosicrucians) to which the visitor claims to belong.</note>, these Wonders of **Omnipotence** <note>original: "Toute-puissance."</note>, and—either after or before all that—to what species of creature <note>The narrator is questioning whether his visitor is a human being, an elemental spirit, or perhaps a demon.</note> I have the honor of speaking."

"You receive me very wisely, Monsieur," he replied, laughing, and taking the armchair I offered him,

->### THE COUNT<-

You ask me first to explain things that I will not tell you today, if you please. The greeting I gave you consists of the words that the **Sages** <term>Sages: In the context of 17th-century occultism, these are "illuminated" masters or initiates who possess secret knowledge of the universe, often associated with the Rosicrucians.</term> say upon meeting those to whom they have resolved to open their hearts and reveal their **Mysteries** <note>original: "Mysteres." These are the secret religious or philosophical doctrines hidden from the uninitiated masses.</note>. I believed that, being as learned as you appeared to be in your letters, this salutation would not be unknown to you, and that it was the most pleasant compliment that the **Count of Gabalis** <note>The title character, an elemental-invoking philosopher from Germany who claims to be a master of the secret sciences.</note> could pay you.

"Ah! Monsieur," I cried, remembering that I had a great role to play <note>The narrator is feigning extreme interest and humility—playing the part of a devoted seeker—to encourage the Count to reveal more of his secrets.</note>, "how shall I make myself worthy of so much kindness? Is it possible that the greatest of all men is in my **study** <note>original: "cabinet" — a private room used for reading, writing, and receiving special guests; the 17th-century equivalent of a home office or laboratory.</note>, and that the great Gabalis honors me with his visit?"

"I am the least of the Sages," replied...

->### CONCERNING GABALIS.<-

I hope that you might equal them one day, if I dare judge by the **figure of your nativity** <term>figure of your nativity: An astrological birth chart. At the time, astrology was considered a serious science used to determine if a person had the temperament required for spiritual enlightenment.</term>, which you did me the honor of sending me: but you will allow me to complain to you, Monsieur," he added laughing, "that you initially took me for a ghost."

"Ah! Not as a ghost," I said to him; "but I confess to you, Monsieur, that suddenly remembering what **Cardan** <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576) was a famous Italian mathematician and occultist. He wrote about a specific event where his father was visited by seven spirits who appeared as men in colorful clothing.</note> relates—how his father was visited one day in his study by seven strangers, dressed in various colors, who spoke to him in quite..."

# THE COUNT

...bizarre in their nature and their occupation . . . . "I understand you," interrupted the Count: "they were **Sylphs** <note>original: "Sylphes." In the lore of this book, Sylphs are the elemental spirits of the Air. The Count views them as actual physical beings, though of a finer matter than humans.</note> of whom I shall tell you one day; they are a species of **aerial substances** <note>original: "ſubſtances aëriennes." This implies they have bodies composed of the element of air.</note>, who sometimes come to consult the **Sages** <note>The "Wise Men" or enlightened initiates of the secret brotherhood to which the Count belongs.</note> regarding the books of **Averroës** <note>Averroës (1126–1198) was an influential medieval philosopher. The Count suggests that even supernatural spirits find his complex commentaries difficult to grasp.</note> which they do not understand very well. **Cardan** is a blunderer for having published that in his *Subtleties* <note>Referring to Cardano's famous work *De subtilitate rerum* (1550), a foundational text of Renaissance natural philosophy.</note>; he had found those notes in the papers of his Father, who was one of us; and who, seeing that his son was a natural chatterbox, did not want to teach him anything of importance, and left him to amuse himself with **ordinary Astrology** <term>ordinary Astrology: The Count distinguishes between the popular, predictive astrology used to tell fortunes and the "sacred" astrology used by initiates for spiritual mastery.</term>, through which he failed to foresee even that his son would be hanged. That rogue is the reason why you did me the insult of taking me for a **Sylph**."

"Insult!" I replied. "What, Monsieur, could I be so unfortunate..."

<summary>The Count identifies Cardano’s mysterious visitors as Sylphs and mocks the philosopher for revealing secret knowledge while failing to protect his own family through his "ordinary" astrology.</summary>

### ->DE GABALIS.<-

—at birth, that he has fifty thousand **livres** <note>original: "livres de rente." The livre was the currency of France until 1795. I understood nothing of all this; yet I resolved to see it through to the end; even though I foresaw that there would be some **Sermons** to endure, and that the **Demon**, which drove him, was greatly **Moral** and a **Preacher**.

# LE COMTE

## SECOND CONVERSATION
### *on the Secret Sciences.*

The Count wished to grant me the entire night to devote myself to **Prayer** <note>original: "Priere." In the hermetic tradition, spiritual purity through prayer was considered a necessary prerequisite for receiving "secret" knowledge.</note>: and the following day, at the break of dawn, he informed me by a **note** <note>original: "billet." A short, informal letter or message.</note> that he would come to my home around eight o'clock; and that, if I were willing, we would go for an excursion together. I waited for him: he arrived, and after the **mutual courtesies** <note>original: "civilités reciproques." Formal etiquette was a defining feature of 17th-century French social interaction, even between those engaged in radical spiritual discussions.</note>; "Let us go," he said to me, "to some place where we are free, and where no one can interrupt our conversation."

"**Ruel**," I said to him, "seems to me quite pleasant and sufficiently secluded." <note>Rueil (modern Rueil-Malmaison) was a town outside Paris famous for its beautiful gardens and the château of the late Cardinal Richelieu, making it a popular destination for private walks.</note>

"Let us go then," he replied. We boarded the **carriage**. During the journey, I observed my new **Master** <term>Master: In esoteric traditions, the teacher-student relationship is often framed as "Master" and "Neophyte" or "Disciple."</term>. I have never noticed in anyone a

# THIRD DIALOGUE

## *on the Secret Sciences.*

---

After having dined, we returned to the labyrinth <note>original: "labyrinte." A common feature in 17th-century formal gardens, the labyrinth here serves as a metaphor for the winding and complex nature of the Count’s occult theories.</note>. I was pensive, and the pity I felt for the Count's extravagance—from which I judged well that it would be difficult to cure him—prevented me from being as amused by everything he had told me as I would have been, had I hoped to bring him back to common sense. I searched within **Antiquity** for something to oppose him with, to which he could not respond; for as for citing the sentiments of the **Church**, he had declared to me that he held only to the ancient religion of his Fathers, the **Philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophes." He gave me the opportunity to broach the subject when, before entering the labyrinth, he turned toward the garden. "This is quite beautiful," he said, "and these statues produce a rather fine effect."

Nothing exists except by this fire, which is God Himself.*

# LE COMTE

one can attribute everything that exceeds human weakness, and which does not approach divine greatness.

This opinion belongs to all of ancient Philosophy. ## FOURTH CONVERSATION.
### on the Secret Sciences.

I WAITED at home for the Count of Gabalis, as we had agreed upon when we parted. He arrived at the appointed hour and, approaching me with a cheerful air, said, "Well, my son, for which species of invisible peoples <note>The Count refers to the four classes of elemental spirits central to his philosophy: Sylphs (air), Gnomes (earth), Nymphs/Undines (water), and Salamanders (fire).</note> has God given you the greatest inclination, and which alliance would you prefer: that of the Salamanders or the Gnomes, the Nymphs or the Sylphs?"

"I have not yet quite decided on this marriage, <note>In the Count's "Cabalistic" system, "marriage" refers to a physical and spiritual union between a human and an elemental spirit. This union is said to grant the spirit an immortal soul and the human extraordinary knowledge.</note> Monsieur," I replied.

"What is holding you back then?" he asked.

"Frankly, Monsieur," I told him, "I cannot cure my imagination; it always represents these supposed inhabitants of the Elements to me as a sort of minor devils." <note>original: "Tiercelets de Diables." A *tiercelet* is a male bird of prey, specifically one-third smaller than the female. Here, the narrator uses the term metaphorically to mean "lesser" or "minor" versions of demons, reflecting his lingering Catholic suspicion of these spirits.</note>

# LETTER
### TO
# MY LORD*** <note>original: "MONSEIGNEUR." This was a formal title of address for high-ranking nobility or members of the royal family in 17th-century France.</note>

MY LORD,

You have always appeared *so* zealous for your Friends, that I believed you would forgive me the liberty I take on behalf of the best of mine, to implore you to have the kindness toward him of having his Book <term>Book: Referring here to the "Entretiens sur les sciences secrètes" or *The Count of Gabalis*.</term> read to you. # L E T T E R

As for the Devotion <term>Devotion: In this context, the outward display of religious piety or fervor which the author suggests is a necessary but perhaps superficial characteristic of Kabbalistic writing.</term> that is almost always present throughout this entire Work, it is an inevitable necessity, he says, that a Kabbalist <term>Cabaliste: A practitioner of the "Kabbalah," a system of esoteric and mystical teachings that the author is treating satirically.</term> speaks of God; but what is fortunate in this matter is that it is of an even more inevitable necessity—in order to preserve the Kabbalistic character—to speak of God only with extreme respect. # RESPONSE.
Part I. Q

...you do me the honor of attributing to me a knowledge that I do not possess; if I have read a few books, it was to see the various opinions held by men, and not to adopt any of them; for I hold fast to little other than this sentiment: that, except for a small number of truths, all things are open to debate <note>The original French uses the term "problématique," which in the 17th century carried a stronger sense of being "uncertain" or "unproven" than the modern English "problematic."</note>. Thus, I am ill-suited to judge the dispute you are having with your friend, the Author. Nevertheless, I am so afraid that you might declare war on me if I refuse to tell you what I think of the book, that I prefer to live in safety, even at the risk of producing a judgment, be it good or bad. If I judge well, it will be a miracle; for as you know: ***every man is a liar*** <note>Latin original: *Omnis homo mendax*. This is a biblical reference to Psalm 116:11, often used in this era to express a skeptical view of human reason and testimony.</note>; if I judge poorly, you will be the reason I did so, and I reserve the right to disavow <note>original: "desavoüer"</note> my opinion whenever I please. In any case, this will be done as a friend, and I shall not...

"This must be one of our distillers <term>Distillateurs: Literally "distillers," this refers to alchemists or early chemists who "distilled" substances to find their quintessence. Literally "the received folds," a metaphorical way of describing inclinations or patterns of thought that have been "impressed" upon a person’s character.</note> impressions they have received begin to smooth out over time, or because their Spirit no longer takes an interest in preserving them, they then perceive with astonishment the irregularity and the folly of their choice.

—barrassed, Gentlemen; Mr. de Sommaise <note>Likely referring to Claude-Barthélemy Bernard, Seigneur de Sommaise, a contemporary figure known for his scholarly interests.</note> is only three steps from here. This plan pleased the assembly. Mr. de Sommaise was sent for, and after some playful introductions, they placed the scrap of paper before his eyes, asking him if he recognized the language <term>idiome</term> of the words presented to him.

"Yes," he replied, "it is Syriac <term>Syriaque</term> <note>original: 'Syriaque'. A dialect of Middle Aramaic historically used by Eastern Christian communities.</note> written in French script."

"But," they said to him, "what do they mean?"

"To provide an accurate translation," he replied, "it must be read as follows: *Leave your house, for it will fall into ruin today at nine o'clock in the evening.*" <note>original: 'fors de ta maison, car elle tombera en ruine aujourd’hui à neuf heures du soir.'</note>

The translation was met with a burst of laughter, and they claimed that the dream was nothing more than a joke and a tall tale.

completely extravagant; the **prophetic dream** <note>original: "le ſonge" — distinguished here from the "rêve" or ordinary dream mentioned on the previous page.</note>, on the contrary, is a sensible inspiration from the **Genius** <term>Génie: In this context, a "Genius" refers to a tutelary spirit or guardian deity believed by the ancients to accompany a person throughout their life.</term>, which leads us toward some good, or which tends to preserve us from some evil. It is on this foundation that I could argue with some **plausibility** <note>original: "quelque couleur" — literally "some color," meaning a pretext or a convincing appearance of truth.</note> that these people owe to the foresight and advice of their **Genii** <note>original: "Génies"</note> all the wise resolutions they have formed, and all the just measures they have taken, to extract themselves from trouble as happily as they did.

But, without dwelling on mere likelihood, I pass, my Son, to certainty; and I am willing to tell you in confidence that it is easy for me to obtain a secret **interview** <note>original: "entretien" — a formal conversation or meeting.</note> with whichever Genius I please.

->## THE ATTENDANTS. 111 <-

Just as they were about to charge one another, a third army arrived and took them both on the flank; by a terrifying noise of musketry and cannon, it put them to flight, dispersed them, and disappeared along with them. Countless **Peasants** saw this, and some even approached these **Troops** so closely that they could describe their faces and their clothing. The report of this **apparition** <note>original: "aparition" — This term refers to a supernatural appearance or vision, often of a spiritual or spectral nature.</note> was soon spread throughout the entire **Province**; nothing else was spoken of in **Nantes** and **Rennes**, which are near the place where this scene was performed. The **Marquis Commandant** (I withhold his name, because of the injustices that have been done to him) was informed of it like the others; he had a sort of **official report** <term>procès verbal: A formal record of a statement or observation made by a public official or notary, used here as evidence of the event.</term> drawn up, and based on this well-articulated report, he composed a narrative which he sent to the **Ministers**.

"But, my Father," I continued, "dare I ask you what limit is assigned to the life of these Genies?"

"The Ancients," he replied, "linked their destiny to ours; and Arnobius <note>Arnobius of Sicca (c. 255–c. 330 AD) was an early Christian apologist. In his work *Adversus Nationes*, he argued against the natural immortality of the soul, suggesting it was a gift from God rather than an inherent quality.</note> does not hesitate to say that they are born and die with us. Note that he was speaking in a time when people were not so meticulous or enlightened concerning the duration of spirits; but today, since our reasoning has been pushed to the infinite, and being aided by Christian Philosophy, we conceive of no cause for their destruction either within them or outside of them, it is a dogma of faith to believe that they are immortal."

<page-num>150</page-num>
# LE GNOME #

Although the Gnome Prince had listened to my harangue with a fair amount of calm, I nonetheless noticed that he was restraining himself <note>original: "se faisoit violence," literally "doing violence to himself," suggesting he was struggling to keep his temper or silence for the sake of politeness.</note> out of a lingering respect he had for Magnamara, who was present at our conversation. Despite their divisions with us, these subterranean Peoples have preserved much esteem and consideration for the Philosophers <note>In the context of 17th and 18th-century "secret sciences," a Philosopher was not just a thinker, but an initiate—specifically an alchemist or a sage who understood the hidden laws of nature and could command elemental spirits.</note>. They obey their commands, and it is very often to the enlightenment they receive from them that they owe their finest knowledge. I do not know who had instructed the Gnome Prince. He did not lack erudition, and aside from his errors—which were great and numerous—one found enough pleasure in hearing him peddle his wares <note>original: "débiter sa marchandise," an idiomatic and somewhat dismissive way of describing someone presenting their arguments or ideas.</note>. As soon as I had ceased speaking, he began to contradict with great fire everything I had put forward regarding the excellence of our nature.

<summary>The Gnome Prince, despite his respect for the philosopher Magnamara, reacts with heated disagreement to the narrator's claims about the superiority of human nature. The text notes the complex relationship between humans (Philosophers) and the elemental spirits who often learn from them.</summary>
<keywords>Gnome Prince, Magnamara, elemental spirits, subterranean peoples, human nature, occult philosophy, erudition</keywords>

"You believe us to be deceived," he said, turning toward me and addressing me; "you believe us to be prejudiced, and yet it is only your own preoccupation <note>original: "préoccupation"; in this context, it refers to a mental bias or a fixed idea that prevents one from seeing the reality of the human condition.</note> that draws you into these unjust sentiments. I admit that at the birth of the world, man was showered with blessings, and that he had the power to share them with us; but his fall <note>A reference to the "Fall of Man" (the lapse from innocence in the Garden of Eden), which in many occult traditions resulted not only in spiritual death but in the loss of magical command over the elemental spirits.</note> has deprived him of all these great advantages, and in losing them, he lost the ability to communicate them to us. You need only look through the Centuries & all the Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to the various established philosophical and theological traditions of academia.</note>, and you will find there an infinity of Sects of Souls armed against one another, & ready to slaughter each other to uphold their extravagances concerning the nature & the quality of their substance <note>original: "étoffe"; literally "fabric" or "stuff," here referring to the underlying material or essence of the soul.</note>. I call the Sects of Philosophers "Sects of Souls," & I do them no wrong; for besides the fact that they spiritualize <note>In this context, to "spiritualize" is to treat oneself as a purely intellectual or non-material being.</note> themselves quite differently from others, it is certain, according to their principles & yours, that their bodies have no part in their reasoning. In truth, Sir, it is a sight to behold these beautiful Souls growing heated & employing all the force of their eloquence & their reason, each to support their differing opinions on what they are. The mere research they conduct with such labor is a proof of their degradation & of their

What would people say of you in the world, if you went into the public squares to ask seriously whether you were Oronte or Lycidas, Tircis or Phylandre <note>These are stock names for characters in classical French literature and pastoral poetry, used here to represent any typical person.</note>, French or Spanish; would they hesitate to condemn you by right to the *Petites Maisons* <note>The "Petites Maisons" was a famous hospital in Paris known for housing the mentally ill; to be sent there was to be declared insane.</note>. Her body is even more enigmatic to her than her own **Nature**. She does not know if it is her **prison**, or her **Kingdom**; <note>This is a classic philosophical dilemma: the Platonic view of the body as a "prison" (soma sema) versus the Aristotelian or more optimistic view of the soul as a rightful ruler of its physical realm.</note> if she existed before it was, or if it existed before she could be. ...that she would work towards its formation and its arrangement, and that she would make herself its governess and Queen; I do not see that these pompous <note>The French *fastueux* implies ostentatious or showy titles that lack substance.</note> titles can acquire much glory for her. Never was a **Sovereign** less obeyed. Never was an **Empire** worse governed. She does not know if she is fixed or wandering. She has never been able to precisely mark the place of her **residence**; and I am persuaded that she is still ignorant of it, and that she will always be ignorant of it. In 18th-century literature, elemental spirits like Gnomes were often depicted as having their own noble hierarchies and were addressed with formal titles.</note> would abandon these views, if you would consider humanity as much from its good side as you have looked at it from the bad. Man is a kind of medal, upon which eyes—both those of friends and enemies—find equally what they seek. Position him well; cast your gaze only upon the most pleasant face of the Medal. You will soon be convinced of the excellence of his nature, and that all the deformity you perceive on the reverse is but a trick and an artifice of Optics <note>In the 18th century, "Optics" was a popular scientific metaphor used to describe how perspective, light, and lenses can distort or reveal the truth of an object.</note>, which never shows objects as they truly are."

<page-num>176</page-num>
<header>THE GNOME</header>

...wise <note>Original: "me"; the conclusion of the word *même* from the preceding page, here meaning "likewise" or "even so."</note>, I said to him, I am persuaded, my Prince, that your Highness would abandon them <note>Referring to the Gnome's cynical opinions about humanity.</note>, if you were willing to consider them from the good side for as long as you have looked at them from the bad. Man is a kind of **medal** <term>medal: in the 18th century, medals and coins were common metaphors for character, having a noble "obverse" (front) and a baser "reverse" (back).</term>, where eyes—whether those of friends or enemies—equally find what they are looking for. If you consider him from a certain point of view, he will appear to you with the air and **Majesty** of a **Sovereign**; but if you change your position, and turn him another way, you will find nothing but a beast. Position him well: focus your gaze only on the most pleasing face of the **Medal**; you will soon be convinced of the excellence of his nature, and that all the deformity you perceive on the reverse is a trick and artifice of **Optics** <note>The science of light and vision; the author suggests that a cynical perspective acts like a distorting lens rather than showing reality.</note>, which never shows objects as they truly are.

<vocab>Prince, Highness, Medal, Sovereign, Optics</vocab>

<summary>The narrator challenges the Gnome's cynicism by comparing human nature to a medal that possesses both a majestic, sovereign face and a beastly reverse. He argues that human "deformity" is often a matter of the viewer's distorted perspective rather than an inherent truth.</summary>

He has been stripped of his **Nobility**, he has fallen from his **Glory**, you say; but whatever his fall may be, his entire perfection did not fall with him: his sin erased some brilliant features of the image of a God <note>The narrator is referencing the *Imago Dei* (Image of God), a theological concept suggesting humans possess a spark of the divine that, according to many thinkers, was tarnished but not destroyed by the "Original Sin."</note> that had been engraved in his soul; but the foundation of this image remained there, and preserved with it the **immortality** that is inseparable from it. One no longer knows what one says, nor what one thinks; one speaks and one reasons...

Because the activity of this fire, being too weak in the former, to set into proper motion those **subtle parts** <note>The author refers to "animal spirits" or corpuscles—extremely fine particles then believed to facilitate communication between the soul and the body.</note> from which the **Soul** is formed, can only produce common minds. This same activity, being in the latter at a point of proportion and perfection where nothing is lacking: its action is full of accuracy, and it necessarily produces good minds. Finally, this activity being beyond measure in the last group, it so agitates and dissipates these small **subtle bodies** <note>original: "petits corps subtils" — small particles of matter that make up the physical substrate of thought.</note> that the **higher illuminations** <note>A term for divine or intellectual insights that were thought to "strike" the mind to produce understanding.</note> always miss their mark, and cannot introduce any reason there;

<summary>The Gnome explains his theory of intelligence, arguing that the "fire of the blood" must be perfectly regulated to move the subtle particles of the soul; too little heat results in dullness, while too much results in incoherent thoughts.</summary>
<keywords>vital heat, soul, subtle parts, animal spirits, physiology of thought, enlightenment, cognition, reason</keywords>

To wish to attribute to them another principle, and to flatter oneself with knowing it, is to form a fanciful idea, and to imitate Pherecydes <note>original: "Pherceïdes" — Pherecydes of Syros (c. 6th century BCE), a Greek thinker often credited by ancient sources as the first to teach the immortality of the human soul.</note>, who was believed to have invented the opinion of the **immortality of Souls** only to satisfy the ambition of his own.

Its life is a sort of ladder <note>Likely an allusion to "Jacob's Ladder," a common motif representing the path of ascent to the divine or descent to the material.</note> that touches **Heaven** at one end and the earth at the other. ...his heart, his **Spirit**, and his everything; and he is not content with simply retracing in him the features of his **faded Image** <note>original: "Image effacée" — The theological concept that the divine likeness in humanity was blurred or obscured by the fall from grace.</note>; he joins him once more to his **Original** <term>Original: Referring here to God as the divine prototype or source from which the human soul was first modeled.</term> for an eternity.

"Allow me," I said to him, "to interrupt you at such a beautiful point; I was waiting for you there. You agree that today the **Soul** is rehabilitated, and that from the carnal and perishable state into which it had fallen, it has been brought back to the **Spirit** and to **Immortality**; why then do you use as a pretext for your withdrawal from men a lack of immortality, from which the **Spirit of the Most High** has delivered them?"

"Because," he replied, "this **Divine Spirit** is found in few people, and those whom he honors with his preference, while recovering the **Privilege** of becoming immortal, do not regain the privilege of communicating themselves to others." <note>The speaker suggests that spiritual restoration is an individual grace that cannot be passed from one person to another like a conversation or a physical gift.</note>

"Adam," I resumed, "had this advantage, then, while he was faithful, and so long as he did not violate that **Law of communication** <term>Law of communication: The theological concept of a natural, direct interaction between humanity and spiritual or divine beings, which was supposedly severed after the Fall of Man.</term> which you claim was given to him along with life?"

...to perceive mystery in the obscurity of his words—mysteries which perhaps he did not even understand himself. It was upon this foundation that they imposed a law upon their **Followers** <note>original: "Sectateurs" — refers to the devoted disciples or adherents of a specific philosophical school.</note> to penetrate the meaning of the **Enigma** he had tossed out at random; this task committed them to such grueling vigils and research that it cost nearly all of them their sanity and their health.

"It is a pity," I said to **Magnamara**, "that the **Record** of this Assembly has not reached us. The **Dissenters** surely should have taken some verified copy with them." "It remained for a long time in the Archives of the **Areopagus**," <note>The Areopagus was the "Hill of Ares" in Athens, serving as a prestigious high court and council of elders.</note> he continued; "but since then, it has been swept up in the revolutions and the ruins of that once-flourishing **Republic**."

...left behind; it is that whatever knowledge Books give us regarding the nature of our **Soul**, and that of its **Author** <note>In this context, the "Author" refers to the Creator or God.</note>, one could not know them perfectly except through the light that one draws from **Religion**. It is there that we must stop, without which we find ourselves in deadly perplexities, and in a continual and fatal wandering. It is necessary that we know that **God** created our Soul, and that He made it for Himself; but it matters little to us to know whether it is a stranger in the body, or if it originates within it; whether it arrives there after the body is formed, or if it helps to form it. ## Partie II.

...number of days; there is more probability that the soul is present at the moment of conception, and that it works toward the miraculous arrangement of all the parts of the body, and toward regulating their internal systems <note>original: "économie" — in historical medicine, this refers to the functional organization and vital processes of a living being.</note> and their functions. The **Physicians of Paris** had the body of a **Lute Player** opened: those who performed this operation maintained that a child, with which she had been pregnant for only three days, had already been animated <note>original: "animé" — literally "given life" or "given a soul," from the Latin *anima*.</note>, and they based their testimony and their conjecture on the fact that the three ventricles <note>In early anatomy, the three ventricles of the brain were believed to be the seats of different mental faculties like sensation, reasoning, and memory.</note> were already formed.

That is all well and good, I said to him, I know too little about **Anatomy** to contest that point. But I have a great desire to know if there are **Souls** that are greater and more noble than others. I believe them all to be equal, he replied to me, since they all

—in us, he compares our **Soul** to the glass of a mirror. The **Divine Word** <term>Divine Word: The "Logos" or the Second Person of the Trinity, through whom Christians believe the world was created</term>, he says, looks at himself in this **mystical Glass**, and produces his **Image** there; and upon this continual gaze, which becomes reciprocal between them, depends the whole being and the entire duration of this **Image** and of the foundation upon which it is imprinted; so that this **Image** would vanish entirely and cease to be, if it ceased for a moment to be looked upon; just as we see the image of a man who looks at himself in a mirror disappear as soon as he moves away and no longer presents himself before it.

Do not be surprised, then, if the **Soul of beasts**, as spiritual as I make it out to be, perishes with their body. # THE GNOME

—asked I, to request the reason why the **Most High**, whose designs and wills are always most equitable, refrained for so long from making for Himself **worshippers**. "I am going to teach it to you," he replied. He began to speak on this subject; but suddenly the words died in his mouth; he became pensive. His eyes seemed fixed to the earth or, to put it better, to the pavement of his cell, and he took on the look and the expression of a man extraordinarily focused. I believed that he was thinking seriously about what he ought to answer me; but it was nothing of the sort. He said to me, as if waking from a deep sleep; "I beg your pardon, my **Son**, if I am obliged to leave you; here is my **Genius** <note>original: "Génie." This refers to a private room used for study, meditation, or receiving important guests.</note>, to..."

doors were useless; for after all, this **Express** <note>original: "Exprès." A specialized messenger or courier sent with speed.</note>, whom I suspected of being **Merlin's courier** <note>A satirical reference to the legendary wizard; the narrator implies the messenger is a magical fabrication or a trick.</note>, could only have entered through the chimney; I concealed my thoughts, and I left to communicate them to **Mr. Schits**, with whom I amused myself greatly over the **role** I had just played. This game continued for several days, after which a letter from the **Count of Schomberg** <note>The Schombergs were a prominent noble family known for their military service across Europe.</note> caused me to return to **Berlin**, there to await the predictions of my true **Genius** <note>The narrator uses "Genius" (génie) here to mean his own destiny or personal guiding spirit, contrasting it with the "spirits" he just encountered.</note>.
